Airport Lounges Are Packed—Travelers Demand $5,000 Annual Credit Card Fees To Keep The Masses Out

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Airport lounges are so crowded that travelers are demanding $200 admission prices and $5,000 annual credit card fees to keep the “untouchables” out, arguing that the “true lounge caste” has been driven into the concourse while everyone else crowds around the buffet. There are also lounges where the line outside and the crowd inside make the benefit nearly worthless. Paying for somewhere comfortable to sit, and then being unable to find a seat, has become a really legitimate complaint – even if it’s mixed with real snobbery because it’s the ‘other’ people who shouldn’t be in there. “Premium” travel experience before 10am on a Sunday https://t.co/13PniAuksG — David Slotnick (@David_Slotnick) November 8, 2026 Status anxiety places airline lounges belong alongside BMWs, remote work, and ski trips as things that have lost their cachet as access spread. They’re still aspirational to the people getting in, while the people already inside are unhappy that increased access has lowered their relative status. View wyatt.today’s post on Twitter The original poster’s follow-up invokes the tens of thousands of his employer’s dollars he spends on flights, then uses a racial slur for other guests. It’s no longer about finding empty space inside. But most people just want a chance to use the benefit they’re paying for and maybe a snack without standing in line in the terminal like a schmuck to get an $895 ‘free’ drink. Wanting Quiet Space Isn’t Elitist One reply argues that a lounge defeats its purpose when it gets too crowded. It may still have food, a shower, or an airline agent to help when your flight gets delayed or cancels. But the core value proposition is escape from the terminal. I often just want a place to open my laptop without balancing it on my knees. And then having frictionless access to a snack, and the ability to run into the washroom without bringing all my stuff with me, are cherries on top. Nobel laureate James Buchanan’s theory of clubs explains this well. Sharing a facility spreads its costs across more people. Eventually, adding people creates enough congestion to make the experience worse. The membership and the size of the facility have to be considered together. There need to be access limits to preserve the value proposition of the facility. But since most people don’t use the space most of the time, it’s perceived option value that they’re purchasing and it continues to drive premium credit card acquisition and adoption. Lounges Got Better, And People Started Using Them Differently I’ve written about the claim that airport lounge culture represents American decline. The nostaglic view is just wrong. I remember ginger ale and Goldfish crackers, a photocopier in the Admirals Club, and packaged Tillamook cheese in United’s Red Carpet Clubs. Today’s better lounges offer actual meals and craft cocktails. When I visited Chase’s new Dallas–Fort Worth lounge, I found the best food inside a bank lounge that isn’t a Capital One Landing. Plus they have whiskey tastings, and facial treatments. The space is 18,000 square feet. These spaces are no longer just a room with seating, snacks and a fax machine. The same improvements help explain why lounges fill up. People go for the food People who might have stopped in to check e-mail now plan to eat a meal. They come more often and stay longer. A better lounge becomes a destination in its own right. The travel mix has changed. Traditional managed business travel is a smaller part of the story. Premium leisure travelers, families, and people combining work with a trip use the same spaces. A Monday – Thursday consultant arriving just before boarding and a family building in a generous airport buffer spend very different amounts of time. Credit cards have expanded access. That brings in more people, including travelers who wouldn’t buy a separate airline club membership. Uncertain security waits get people to the airport earlier. If security might take ten minutes or an hour, you allow for the hour. When it takes ten minutes, you’ve suddenly got time to spend. Even without an increase in the average security wait, greater uncertainty can mean more time in the airport. I laid out these problems for Delta years ago. Their more substantial food draws people in and keeps them there. And Delta doesn’t just admit customers with its own premium card – American Express Platinum cardmembers flying Delta can enter, too. Platinum’s standard allotment is now 10 visits and Delta Reserve’s is 15, with a spending route to unlimited access. Delta also limits entry before an originating flight to three hours prior to scheduled departure, with an exception for connections. A three-hour entry window still leaves plenty of time for a long visit. And if everyone arrives before the same bank of departures, an empty room earlier in the day doesn’t help. The number of people holding an eligible card isn’t the only number that matters. Double the average length of a visit, with the same flow of arrivals, and you double the average number of people occupying the lounge. I recently catalogued 70 new and expanded lounge projects. More space helps, but a new lounge also attracts people who previously skipped the experience. I’d underscore that which lounges we’re talking about matters a great deal. Spending $895 for a Platinum Card doesn’t on its own signal discernment, although I their Salt Lake lounge is quite nice. Capital One’s Landings at Washington National and LaGuardia offer genuinely good food and reservations. You enter and are shown to a table. That’s a totally different experience. You Could Buy Your Way Into Lounges In The 1960s, Too One commenter remembers the 1990s as a time when people earned their way in by flying. But American Airlines started selling lounge memberships in 1967, at $25 a year or $250 for life. Before that, their sales department handed out memberships. Paid lounge access was a function of the civil rights era – a nondiscriminatory way to determine who could enter. The claim that credit card lounges are bad while airline lounges are good doesn’t make sense. I’d happily choose a good Chase or Capital One lounge over plenty of airline clubs. And finding quiet lounges overseas doesn’t mean most overseas lounges are quiet. Have you ever tried to enter a Priority Pass lounge at Heathrow? Comfort Can Spread Even When Status Can’t The discussion of aspiration is revealing. Some people feel that accessibility is the inverse of aspiration – that’s true if what you’re aspiring to is being above other people. But you can also just aspire to a more comfortable trip, get it, and be pleased that other people can get it too. A product can also become less distinctive to its old customers while becoming an attainable aspiration for new ones. The people entering and the people complaining aren’t necessarily the same people. That’s why the comparison to remote work is revealing – someone getting to work from home doesn’t take away the time you save on your commute working from home. Treating it as a status symbol entails a tradeoff (‘when everyone has status, nobody does’). I’m glad more people can have a better trip. I also want the benefit I’ve paid for to be usable. I don’t usually bother with Centurion lounges, they aren’t as nice as Capital One and Chase lounges in many airports and there are too many people waiting to get in. When there’s a choice, I’ll pick elsewhere. But some of those Chase (Las Vegas! Phoenix!) and Capital One lounges (Denver!) can make the wait not worth the squeeze, too. Banks are selling more access than they can deliver. Sometimes I’ll just take the restaurant in the terminal. Of course most banks stopped paying for Priority Passs swipes at restaurants – which would be fair enough if they could accommodate travelers in their own lounges. Double the average length of a visit, with the same flow of arrivals, and you double the average number of people occupying the lounge. I recently catalogued 70 new and expanded lounge projects. More space helps, but a new lounge also attracts people who previously skipped the experience. I’d underscore that which lounges we’re talking about matters a great deal. Spending $895 for a Platinum Card doesn’t on its own signal discernment, although I their Salt Lake lounge is quite nice. Capital One’s Landings at Washington National and LaGuardia offer genuinely good food and reservations. You enter and are shown to a table. That’s a totally different experience. You Could Buy Your Way Into Lounges In The 1960s, Too One commenter remembers the 1990s as a time when people earned their way in by flying. But American Airlines started selling lounge memberships in 1967, at $25 a year or $250 for life. Before that, their sales department handed out memberships. Paid lounge access was a function of the civil rights era – a nondiscriminatory way to determine who could enter. The claim that credit card lounges are bad while airline lounges are good doesn’t make sense. I’d happily choose a good Chase or Capital One lounge over plenty of airline clubs. And finding quiet lounges overseas doesn’t mean most overseas lounges are quiet. Have you ever tried to enter a Priority Pass lounge at Heathrow? Comfort Can Spread Even When Status Can’t The discussion of aspiration is revealing. Some people feel that accessibility is the inverse of aspiration – that’s true if what you’re aspiring to is being above other people. But you can also just aspire to a more comfortable trip, get it, and be pleased that other people can get it too. A product can also become less distinctive to its old customers while becoming an attainable aspiration for new ones. The people entering and the people complaining aren’t necessarily the same people. That’s why the comparison to remote work is revealing – someone getting to work from home doesn’t take away the time you save on your commute working from home. Treating it as a status symbol entails a tradeoff (‘when everyone has status, nobody does’). I’m glad more people can have a better trip. I also want the benefit I’ve paid for to be usable. I don’t usually bother with Centurion lounges, they aren’t as nice as Capital One and Chase lounges in many airports and there are too many people waiting to get in. When there’s a choice, I’ll pick elsewhere. But some of those Chase (Las Vegas! Phoenix!) and Capital One lounges (Denver!) can make the wait not worth the squeeze, too. Banks are selling more access than they can deliver. Sometimes I’ll just take the restaurant in the terminal. Of course most banks stopped paying for Priority Passs swipes at restaurants – which would be fair enough if they could accommodate travelers in their own lounges. Topics on this pageAirport loungeCredit cardAmerican AirlinesAmerican ExpressBMWCapital OneChaseChase BankDavid SlotnickDelta Air LinesDenverHeathrow AirportLaGuardia AirportLas VegasPriority PassTillamookUnited Airlines+15 more More From View from the Wing

I’ve written about the claim that airport lounge culture represents American decline. The nostaglic view is just wrong. I remember ginger ale and Goldfish crackers, a photocopier in the Admirals Club, and packaged Tillamook cheese in United’s Red Carpet Clubs.

Today’s better lounges offer actual meals and craft cocktails. When I visited Chase’s new Dallas–Fort Worth lounge, I found the best food inside a bank lounge that isn’t a Capital One Landing. Plus they have whiskey tastings, and facial treatments. The space is 18,000 square feet. These spaces are no longer just a room with seating, snacks and a fax machine.

The same improvements help explain why lounges fill up.

I laid out these problems for Delta years ago. Their more substantial food draws people in and keeps them there. And Delta doesn’t just admit customers with its own premium card – American Express Platinum cardmembers flying Delta can enter, too. Platinum’s standard allotment is now 10 visits and Delta Reserve’s is 15, with a spending route to unlimited access. Delta also limits entry before an originating flight to three hours prior to scheduled departure, with an exception for connections.

A three-hour entry window still leaves plenty of time for a long visit. And if everyone arrives before the same bank of departures, an empty room earlier in the day doesn’t help. The number of people holding an eligible card isn’t the only number that matters. Double the average length of a visit, with the same flow of arrivals, and you double the average number of people occupying the lounge.

I recently catalogued 70 new and expanded lounge projects. More space helps, but a new lounge also attracts people who previously skipped the experience.

I’d underscore that which lounges we’re talking about matters a great deal. Spending $895 for a Platinum Card doesn’t on its own signal discernment, although I their Salt Lake lounge is quite nice. Capital One’s Landings at Washington National and LaGuardia offer genuinely good food and reservations. You enter and are shown to a table. That’s a totally different experience.

And finding quiet lounges overseas doesn’t mean most overseas lounges are quiet. Have you ever tried to enter a Priority Pass lounge at Heathrow? The discussion of aspiration is revealing. Some people feel that accessibility is the inverse of aspiration – that’s true if what you’re aspiring to is being above other people. But you can also just aspire to a more comfortable trip, get it, and be pleased that other people can get it too.

A product can also become less distinctive to its old customers while becoming an attainable aspiration for new ones. The people entering and the people complaining aren’t necessarily the same people.

That’s why the comparison to remote work is revealing – someone getting to work from home doesn’t take away the time you save on your commute working from home. Treating it as a status symbol entails a tradeoff (‘when everyone has status, nobody does’).

I’m glad more people can have a better trip. I also want the benefit I’ve paid for to be usable. I don’t usually bother with Centurion lounges, they aren’t as nice as Capital One and Chase lounges in many airports and there are too many people waiting to get in. Mamdani gives nonprofits first dibs on buying apartment buildings

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Mayor Zohran Mamdani signed COPA, requiring owners to notify the city and qualified nonprofits before selling covered buildings. The law takes effect in 1 year, as a 38 building portfolio with 850 mostly rent stabilized units heads to foreclosure auctions.  AI Summary

New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani signed legislation Tuesday giving vetted nonprofits a first shot at buying troubled city apartment buildings.

The new measure arrived too late to spare hundreds of rent-stabilized units from foreclosure, a second loss in Mamdani’s bid to protect such units.

Mamdani ran for office and won on creating and protecting affordable housing. National attention has since focused on his efforts to build more housing and cut red tape. His political inspiration and influence have spread to other parts of the country.

Nithya Raman is a prime example. Under a similar affordability theme, the Los Angeles City Council member, also a member of the Democratic Socialists of America, ousted an incumbent to become L.A. mayor.

The Community Opportunity to Purchase Act, known as COPA, requires owners of covered buildings to notify the city and qualified nonprofits before selling. Those groups then get time to make an offer and a right to match a later one. The law takes effect one year after enactment, after the Department of Housing Preservation and Development writes rules.

“This law will give New Yorkers a tool to keep their homes out of the hands of predatory investors seeking to make a quick and easy buck,” Mamdani said at a signing event Tuesday.

He also won in court last week. A Brooklyn appellate panel ruled Nov. 4 that the administration can resume collecting its pied-à-terre tax on high-end second homes, reversing a Staten Island judge who found the rollout flawed. Mamdani says the tax could raise $500 million a year. Other legal challenges remain.

But his administration could not stop the sale of a 38-building portfolio with 850 mostly rent-stabilized apartments. Spatial Equity, an affordable housing developer with City Hall’s backing, failed to close a deal to buy the portfolio before an East Harlem auction began Tuesday afternoon.

The 38 buildings belonged to bankrupt Emerald Equities Group. Banks and other institutions that control about $250 million in mortgage loans will foreclose on each building over three auction days.

Lenders rejected Spatial Equity’s initial $68 million bid, according to a City Limits report. They also turned down a $110 million offer last month, tenant leader Cara Polte told Gothamist.

Polte said tenants will keep pushing for a buyer committed to fixing the buildings. Tenants had hoped to turn her building and four others on the block into a community land trust.

A trust managed by several banks and by Sabal Investment Holdings controls the loans. Freddie Mac partially insured them. Sabal has yet to respond to media requests.

Cea Weaver, director of the Mayor’s Office to Protect Tenants, said the sale does not end the city’s effort to force owners to improve conditions.

This setback follows Mamdani’s first loss on this front. On Jan. 1, he stood in a Pinnacle-owned building and vowed to take on bad landlords. He then sought a 30-day delay of the bankruptcy auction of Pinnacle’s rent-regulated portfolio, hoping to find a buyer other than Summit Properties USA.

A judge allowed the sale to proceed. Summit won about 5,100 mostly rent-stabilized units from Pinnacle, which had filed for Chapter 11 after Flagstar Bank foreclosed.

Emerald bought the 38 buildings for $350 million in 2016 and began raising rents on stabilized apartments. It took out another $180 million loan in December 2018, six months before state lawmakers passed tenant protections that outlawed many of those tactics.

Lenders foreclosed in 2024. Emerald filed for bankruptcy a year later.

A state investigation found Emerald had illegally removed 21 apartments from rent stabilization. The buildings had more than 2,300 housing code violations in April. A retaining wall behind buildings on East 103rd Street collapsed earlier this year, days before Mamdani and Weaver visited in March.

About 1,600 rent-stabilized buildings face financial distress, according to Rent Guidelines Board data. Landlord groups blame rent restrictions, including the board’s two-year rent freeze. Tenant advocates say owners like Emerald borrowed heavily expecting rent increases that later law barred.

In June, Mamdani won a rent freeze through the Rent Guidelines Board for the city’s roughly 1 million stabilized apartments.

Landlords sued to reverse the freeze. The case began in Staten Island, but Justice Ralph Porzio moved it to Manhattan in August, handing Mamdani a partial win. In September, the judge ordered City Hall to turn over communications with the board and signaled he would likely not rule by Oct. 1.

Oct. 1 came and went without a ruling, and the freeze took effect for new and renewing leases.

Meanwhile, a federal lawsuit remains pending over a 2019 state law that limits rents on stabilized units. Small property owners sued in November 2025, arguing the law unconstitutionally keeps stabilized apartments vacant.

COPA’s path was uneven until Mamdani took office. The City Council passed an earlier version in December 2025, but then-Mayor Eric Adams vetoed it, and the Council did not override him. A revised, narrower version passed Oct. 22.

“Progress … is something that we celebrate, and also, we know that it is not inevitable,” Mamdani said. “It has to be won.”

Pablo Neruda on How to Hold Time

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Every month, I spend hundreds of hours and thousands of dollars keeping The Marginalian going. For two decades, it has remained free, ad-free, AI-free, fully human and alive thanks to patronage from readers. I have no staff, no interns, not even an assistant — a thoroughly one-woman labor of love that is also my life and my livelihood. If it makes your own life more livable in any way, please consider aiding its sustenance with a one-time or loyal donation. Your support makes all the difference.

You can also become a spontaneous supporter with a one-time donation in any amount:

Partial to Bitcoin? You can beam some bit-love my way: 197usDS6AsL9wDKxtGM6xaWjmR5ejgqem7

Need to cancel a recurring donation? Go here.

For an act of resistance to the tyranny of algorithms, try the Marginalian newsletter—undistracted notes on the search for meaning, free, ad-free, AI-free, fully human since 2006. (Here is an example.)

“Time is a river that sweeps me along, but I am a river,” Borges wrote. “Time is a fire that consumes me, but I am the fire.”

Most of us are not Borges. Most of us are drowning in bewilderment at where the time goes, burning with the urgency of being alive while waiting to start living, wandering the labyrinth of life with wayward presence, wishing that time ran differently as the cult of productivity turns each minute into a blade pressed against the vein of our transience.

And all the while, our time is nested within our times — the epoch we are living through together, born into it with no more choice in the matter than the body and brain and family we have been born into. In his magnificent essay on Shakespeare, James Baldwin countered the commonplace lament of every epoch: “It is said that his time was easier than ours, but I doubt it — no time can be easy if one is living through it.” A century before him — a century of unrest and transformation — Emerson issued the ultimate antilamentation: “This time, like all times, is a very good one, if we but know what to do with it.”

Not knowing what to do with the time we have been given, not knowing how to hold time in our personal and political lives, is at bottom an act of forgetting how time hold us. Pablo Neruda (July 12, 1904–September 23, 1973) casts a spell against forgetting in the fourth canto of his long poem “Morning,”:

You will remember that whimsical ravine where the vibrant aromas rose, and from time to time a bird dressed in water and languor: winter’s garment.

You will remember those gifts from the earth: piquing fragrance, gold clay, thickets of herbs, wild roots, bewitching thorns like swords.

You will remember the bouquet you brought, a bouquet of shadow and silent water, a bouquet like foam-covered stone.

And that time was like never and always: We go where nothing is expected and find everything waiting there.

If time is the fundamental problem of human life and poetry is our most precise technology for parsing the aching astonishment of being alive, then time is the prime subject of poetry. Neruda knew this — time is the subterranean current coursing beneath his vast and varied body of work, the substrate upon which all of his stunning love poems and his meditations on the inner life grow. He reverenced the stones for how they have “touched time,” reverenced the minute for how it is “bound to join the river of time that bears us,” reverenced “the inexhaustible springs of time,” longed for “a time complete as an ocean,” then made that ocean with his poetry.

In his poem “The Enigmas,” composed during WWII, he writes:

You’ve asked me what the crustacean spins between its gold claws and I reply: the sea knows.

You wonder what the sea squirt waits for in its transparent bell?      What does it wait for?

I’ll tell you: it’s waiting for time like you.

A decade later, in one of his “Elemental Odes,” Neruda laid out his most explicit instruction for how to hold time:

Listen and learn. Time is divided into two rivers: one flows backward, devouring life already lived; the other moves forward with you exposing your life. For a single second they may be joined. Now. This is that moment, the drop of an instant that washes away the past. It is the present. It is in your hands. Racing, slipping, tumbling like a waterfall. But it is yours. Help it grow with love, with firmness, with stone and flight, with resounding rectitude, with purest grains, the most brilliant metal from your heart, walking in the full light of day without fear of truth, goodness, justice, companions of song, time that flows will have the shape and sound of a guitar, and when you want to bow to the past, the singing spring of transparent time will reveal your wholeness. Time is joy.

Couple with three poems for trusting time, then revisit Kahlil Gibran on how to befriend time.

Every month, I spend hundreds of hours and thousands of dollars keeping The Marginalian going. For two decades, it has remained free, ad-free, AI-free, fully human and alive thanks to patronage from readers. I have no staff, no interns, not even an assistant — a thoroughly one-woman labor of love that is also my life and my livelihood. If it makes your own life more livable in any way, please consider aiding its sustenance with a one-time or loyal donation. Your support makes all the difference.

You can also become a spontaneous supporter with a one-time donation in any amount:

Partial to Bitcoin? You can beam some bit-love my way: 197usDS6AsL9wDKxtGM6xaWjmR5ejgqem7

Need to cancel an existing donation? (It's okay — life changes course. I treasure your kindness and appreciate your support for as long as it lasted.) You can do so on this page.

For an act of resistance to the tyranny of algorithms, try the Marginalian newsletter—undistracted notes on the search for meaning, free, ad-free, AI-free, fully human since 2006. (Here is an example.)

Alex Baena urges Arsenal star to join Atletico Madrid: ‘He’s the second best in the world’

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Alex Baena has urged Atletico Madrid to sign Martin Zubimendi and believes the Arsenal midfielder is the second best in the world behind Rodri.

Zubimendi joined Arsenal in a £60 million deal from Real Sociedad last year and the 27-year-old played an integral role in the team’s Premier League title success under Mikel Arteta last season.

However, Zubimendi is yet to start a Premier League game this term and now faces increased competition for a place in Arsenal’s midfield following the arrival of Bruno Guimaraes in the summer transfer window.

Zubimendi has been linked with a move away from Arsenal ahead of the January transfer window and Chelsea are understood to have registered their interest.

Baena, who was also part of Spain’s World Cup success in the summer alongside Zubimendi, is keen to see his countryman return to La Liga.

When asked about the prospect of Zubimendi joining Atletico, Baena told Cadena SER: ‘I would love for him to come.

‘He is the second best defensive midfielder in the world after Rodri.

‘I hope he can come. He would be a great addition to the team and the dressing room.’

Baena also spoke out on Arsenal’s summer transfer target Julian Alvarez, who pushed for a move to Barcelona but ended up staying at Atletico and received hostility from the club’s supporters.

‘But I think he’s working very hard, trying to get back to his old self. I think if he keeps this up, people will grow to like him more.’

A Look Back at the Times Kristen Bell and Dax Shepard Defended Their Marriage

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Kristen Bell and Dax Shepard have never been shy about the realities of their relationship.

Since tying the knot in 2013, the couple — who share daughters Lincoln and Delta — have weathered public scrutiny over everything from controversial social media posts to celebrity hot takes about their pairing. Through it all, both Bell and Shepard have consistently shown up for each other and spoken candidly about the work that goes into their marriage.

Keep reading for a look back at the key moments, from most recent to earliest:

Bell and Shepard became the subject of online discourse for the third time in a year when the Nobody Wants This star revealed the surprising way her husband flirts with her: complimenting other people.

“If we go to a dinner or something, like, there’s a couple things Dax does where he doesn’t have to look up and wink at me, but I know that he’s just being the best boy,” she said during an October 2026 episode of Shepard’s podcast. “We’ll be at dinner, and, like, he’ll say to the waiter or waitress, he’ll go, ‘Wow, you have incredible eyelashes, gorgeous eyes, great job.’”

Bell insisted that this is a form of flirting with her because she loves that “he just made someone else feel good.”

“‘Cause he’s gonna have zero interaction with — let’s say it is a girl. He’s gonna have zero interaction with this girl for the rest of his life,” she continued. “The chances of him ever having another conversation with her are so microscopic. But to me, to know that my partner actively smiles at people and gives them compliments [makes me feel good].”

Days after the clip went viral, Bell gushed about his “commitment to growth” during an appearance on The Drew Barrymore Show.

“He’s committed to growth in all aspects,” she said, adding that he’s a “lifelong learner.”

Bell continued, “I call him a vulneraboy. Because he is vulnerable. He knows a lot of stuff, but he’s also not afraid or ashamed to say, ‘I don’t know, I’ve never experienced this before.’ And that’s very sexy.”

Shepard broke his silence on the flirting claims during a November 2026 episode of his podcast, calling out the “weird narrative” spreading online and painting him as “controlling, abusive, now flirting with people in public, which I don’t do.”

“We’re saying I flirt with Kristen by tipping people a lot, and I flirt with Kristen. Yeah, by complimenting people. If you think I’m complimenting hot young waitresses, you got a problem, right?” he explained. “I’m finding the dude that I don’t think has had a compliment in a while and telling him I love his shoes. It’s always somebody that I want to make their day. And so those compliments [are] flirting with Kristen. … I will offer publicly $10 million to any server you can find that I have given my number to, or hit on.”

Shepard said he found the narrative “deeply insulting” to Bell because it implies that she allows emotional abuse around their daughters, Delta and Lincoln. He also noted that he’s “dedicated his whole life” to breaking the cycle of abuse he grew up around.

“I don’t ever yell at my children,” he said. “I don’t swear at them. I don’t yell at my wife. You know, like, to have given it my all to be the opposite of what now some group of people is saying I am is hard, of course. So hard. I don’t love it.”

Shepard concluded, “Generally, I can brush [these controversies] off, but when we’re now on year two of now, there’s a new [one] … then it just starts making me think like, ‘Oh, is this now my f***ing role going forward is just, like, every four months there’s another thing about me being a terrible man. And that makes me want to go away.”

Nearly six months after Bell stirred up controversy with a wedding anniversary tribute, Shepard finally broke his silence on the drama during the Monday, April 6, episode of his “Armchair Expert” podcast with guest Nikki Glaser.

“I was alerted by someone that this whole thing was happening,” Shepard recalled, brushing off the criticism surrounding their marriage after critics of Bell’s October 2025 Instagram post suggested that she walk away and leave their relationship for good. “I didn’t know, and Kristen knew through her publicist but didn’t tell me. So, I actually didn’t know, and this was brought to my attention.”

Shepard faced backlash after Bell gushed over “the man who once said to me: ‘I would never kill you.’ A lot of men have killed their wives at a certain point. Even though I’m heavily incentivized to kill you, I never would.” She shared the controversial message in honor of the special occasion.

Glaser revealed she had initially planned to poke fun at the situation during the 2026 Golden Globe Awards in January 2026, but cut it because she didn’t know where Shepard stood on the topic.

As she prepared to host The Actors Awards, Bell gushed about her husband in an interview with People, noting that he uplifts her spirits and never tries to put her down.

“There’s nothing that he won’t do to take care of me,” Bell said. “One thing that I love so much about being married to him is that he is never allergic to me shining more and more. He never gets insecure about it.”

She added that being married for over a decade is a “long time,” noting, “We put a lot of work into it and I’m very happy. We’re very supportive of each other and our marriage and our family.”

Cher told Shepard and Bell to their faces that she thought Bell could “do better” in terms of finding a romantic partner during a January 2026 appearance on Shepard’s “Armchair Expert” podcast. The Good Place actress didn’t let it go to her head, instead stating that Shepard was “almost too good” for her, telling them both it was a “slow burn” that’s lasted.

During the press tour for season 2 of Nobody Wants This, Bell drew parallels between her marriage and the show’s central romance. “I feel like my real-life husband and I are ‘opposites attract,'” she told People, noting they have grown stronger as a couple despite facing constant scrutiny in the public eye.

That was the same month Bell’s 12th wedding anniversary post to Shepard sparked backlash. The National Network to End Domestic Violence responded to her controversial post, telling Page Six, “There’s nothing funny about domestic violence ‘jokes’ that trivialize the very real fear, trauma and pain that millions of victims and survivors face each day.”

During a conversation with Alicia Silverstone on “Armchair Expert,” Shepard declared he would never speak negatively about his marriage with Bell, even hypothetically.

“I say this all the time, like, if Kristen and I ever split up, I would only describe this as still an enormously successful relationship,” Shepard said. “Like, we loved each other to death. We had these two beautiful children. We had this great time together.”

During an appearance on The View, Bell defended Shepard’s second date move of taking gum from her mouth and chewing it — a story that “divided the hosts” and audience.

“I personally thought it was very cute and it was the indicator, after we had been talking and chatting for awhile and I was like, ‘Oh, this guy’s kinda cute and special,'” Bell said. “And then I guess I was being flirty and took it out of my mouth and then he took it. And I was like, ‘Well…’ I said, ‘Understood.'”

Bell set the record straight after seeing a negative remark about herself and Shepard. “Every piece of news I see about them talks about how they just can’t f***ng stand each other, constantly fight, now this dude is apparently objectifying her as well. just what’s the point?” a social media user wrote via Instagram in February 2021.

Bell fired back at the commenter, “We adore each other, we just try to always be honest about how marriage, or companionship in any form, is hard sometimes.” She added, “You cant always be in control, or right, and its important to us that we lead with the honesty of your ‘ perfect match ‘ being a myth. You gotta work hard to love yourself, and love other humans. Xo.”
